But that thought feels stuck to me, like I can’t separate myself from it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something you agree with, or do they go against how you truly feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to agree with it. That’s the hard part. If you ask me what I truly believe about who I used to be, I would’ve said I was careful. I took pride in being the guy who knew what he was doing, who checked his work, who didn’t cut corners. Especially as an electrician, you can’t be sloppy. People trust you with things they don’t even see behind the walls.\n\nBut now... it feels like part of me does agree with it, or at least I can’t argue it down. That’s why I came in. I can’t shake this thought that if all those years doing electrical work, all that training and experience, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not safe to be around in that way. Maybe I’m a danger to anybody working with me, or anybody living under the same roof as me.\n\nAnd I hate saying that, because it goes against the person I thought I was. I don’t want that to be true. I don’t want to believe I’m dangerous. But the thought feels... convincing. Like there’s evidence for it that I can’t get around. So it’s both, I guess. It goes against how I want to see myself, but right now it feels like something I can’t stop believing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are in line with your own values and beliefs about yourself?\n\nPatient: No... I mean, not in line with the values I thought I had, anyway. I never wanted to be someone people had to be afraid of. My whole working life, being an electrician, I tried to be the opposite of that. Careful. Responsible. The person who checks things because people are depending on him.\n\nBut that’s what makes it so hard to answer, because now the thought feels like it’s attached to me. I came in because I can’t shake it — that if all my years doing this work, all the training and experience, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not the careful person I believed I was. Maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works beside me, or anybody who lives under my roof.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t match what I want my values to be. It goes against everything I used to believe about myself. But it feels like this awful belief has moved in and started acting like it belongs there, like it’s the truth whether I want it or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any specific actions to try and neutralize or counteract these thoughts about being a danger to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a specific action, exactly. Not like there’s one ritual I have to do in a certain order or something like that. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by neutralize.\n\nIt’s more... I get caught trying to prove to myself that I’m not dangerous, but I don’t know that I have a set thing I do that makes the thought go away. I’ll think through it over and over, like reviewing what kind of person I am, what I would or wouldn’t do, whether I can trust my own judgment. But I can’t say there’s some clear action where I think, “If I do this, then the thought is canceled out.”\n\nI guess I’m having trouble answering because the thoughts feel so constant that I’m always responding to them in some way, even if it’s just in my head. But no, I haven’t noticed any specific thing I have to perform to counteract it. It’s more like I’m stuck arguing with myself, and the argument never really ends.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions over and over to try and make these thoughts about danger go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying it, like repeating an action over and over until the thought goes away. I haven’t noticed myself doing something like checking a door ten times or touching something a certain number of times, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhat happens more is I get stuck in my head. I’ll go over the same question again and again — am I dangerous, can people trust me, did I miss something about myself — but it’s not like there’s a repeated action that makes it stop. There isn’t some thing I do where I feel relief afterward. If anything, I keep trying to reason with it, and it just circles back.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m repeating certain actions. It’s more like the thoughts repeat themselves, and I keep getting pulled into answering them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are forcing themselves into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what it feels like. I’m not sitting there trying to think it up, and I don’t want it there. It just pushes its way in, over and over.\n\nI came in because I can’t shake this thought that if all my years as an electrician — all the experience, all the safety habits, all the times I’ve checked and double-checked things — couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? It’s like my mind takes that and turns it into, “You’re a danger.” Not just at work, either. A danger to anybody who works beside me, anybody depending on me, anybody under my roof.\n\nAnd I don’t want to believe that. I really don’t. But it feels like the thought doesn’t care what I want. It just shows up and sits there like it has proof, like it’s forcing me to look at it even when I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to mentally replay or reanalyze situations to try and make these thoughts about being a danger to others go away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do it to make the thoughts go away, exactly. I do replay things in my head, but I don’t know if it works like that, like I think, “If I analyze this enough, then I’ll be okay.” It’s more like I get pulled into it before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nI’ll go back over situations, conversations, things I did or didn’t do, and try to figure out if there was some sign I missed or some moment that proves I’m the problem. But it doesn’t really settle anything. It’s not like I reanalyze it and then feel clean or safe afterward. If anything, I can go through it ten different ways and still end up right back at the same question.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do mentally replay and reanalyze, but I don’t know that I do it as a deliberate way to make the thoughts disappear. It’s more like my mind won’t let the subject drop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts about being a danger to others, to try and prevent any harm from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think I have some set thing I do where I think, “If I do this, then nobody will get hurt.” Not like a rule or a ritual, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nWhen the thought hits, I do feel this pressure in my chest, like I need to respond somehow, like I can’t just ignore it because ignoring it feels irresponsible. But I don’t know that I could point to one specific action I always take. It’s more that I get caught trying to figure out whether I’m safe to be around, whether I can trust myself, whether I’m missing something obvious that everyone else can’t see.\n\nSo… I guess I feel like I should do something, but I don’t really know what that “something” is. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where I do an action and then feel like harm has been prevented. It’s more just this awful sense that I need to make absolutely sure nothing bad happens, and I don’t know how to get that certainty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are in conflict with your own self-image?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s part of what makes it so hard to live with, I think. Because I never saw myself that way. I’ve always thought of myself as careful, responsible — especially with electrical work. That’s not a job where you get to be casual. You build your whole way of working around not taking chances, checking things, thinking ahead, making sure the people around you are safe.\n\nAnd now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years of experience, all that training and caution, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe that means something terrible about me. Like maybe I’m not the safe person I thought I was. Maybe I’m actually a danger to people who work beside me, or people who live under my roof.\n\nSo yes, it’s in conflict with how I want to see myself — how I used to see myself. But the thought feels so strong that it’s like it’s trying to rewrite that whole image of who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to neutralize or counteract these thoughts about being a danger to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “neutralize.” I’m not even totally sure what that would look like for me.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific thing I do, like I say a phrase or check something a certain number of times and then the thought gets canceled out. I don’t really have that kind of clear before-and-after feeling. It’s more messy than that. The thought comes in — that I could be dangerous, or that I already missed something important — and then I feel like I have to somehow answer it. Like I can’t just leave it sitting there unchallenged, because if I leave it alone, it feels like I’m accepting that it might be true.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with it, I guess. I’ll think through what happened, what I know about myself, whether I’m actually that kind of person, whether there’s evidence for it or against it. But I don’t know if that counts as counteracting it, because it doesn’t really make it stop. Sometimes it just turns into another loop, where I’m trying to prove to myself I’m not a danger, but every answer has some little “what if” attached to it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel the urge to do something in my head, to push back against it somehow. But I don’t have a specific thing that neutralizes it. If anything, I’m usually left still unsure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something you'd be able to stop thinking about if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could. I’ve tried to just tell myself, “Stop thinking about it, move on, you know who you are,” but it doesn’t work like that.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shake this thought that if all my years doing electrical work — all the training, all the habits, all the ways I thought I knew how to keep things safe — if that still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? It starts turning into this thing where I think, maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Or anybody who lives under the same roof as me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds… I don’t know, extreme maybe. But it doesn’t feel like a thought I can just set down because I want to. It keeps coming back, and when it comes back it feels important, like I’m not allowed to ignore it. Like ignoring it would be careless. So no, I don’t feel like I can just stop thinking about it on command. If I could, I don’t think I’d be sitting here talking about it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are consistent with your own beliefs and values about yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not really. That’s what makes it feel so wrong and so painful. It doesn’t fit with what I used to believe about myself at all.\n\nI’ve spent my whole working life trying to be the opposite of dangerous. As an electrician, you don’t survive by being careless, and you definitely don’t keep other people safe by being reckless. I took pride in knowing what I was doing, checking things, thinking ahead, not cutting corners. I believed I was someone people could trust around that kind of work.\n\nBut now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years doing the job — all the experience and caution and training — still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not who I thought I was. Maybe I’m not safe to be around. Maybe I’m a danger to anyone who works with me, or even people living under the same roof as me.\n\nSo no, it’s not consistent with my values. My values are to protect people, to be responsible, to not put anyone at risk. But the thought keeps coming in anyway, like it’s arguing that none of that matters because something terrible still happened. And even though part of me knows that’s not how I want to see myself, another part of me keeps asking, “What if it’s true?”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think that’s exactly the hard part. I can recognize, at least part of me can, that this isn’t how I used to think about myself. It doesn’t feel like one of my actual values or beliefs, like something I would calmly say is true if I were thinking straight.

But I don’t know that I could point to one specific action I always take. It’s more that I get caught trying to figure out whether I’m safe to be around, whether I can trust myself, whether I’m missing something obvious that everyone else can’t see.\n\nSo… I guess I feel like I should do something, but I don’t really know what that “something” is. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where I do an action and then feel like harm has been prevented. It’s more just this awful sense that I need to make absolutely sure nothing bad happens, and I don’t know how to get that certainty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are in conflict with your own self-image?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s part of what makes it so hard to live with, I think. Because I never saw myself that way. I’ve always thought of myself as careful, responsible — especially with electrical work. That’s not a job where you get to be casual. You build your whole way of working around not taking chances, checking things, thinking ahead, making sure the people around you are safe.\n\nAnd now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years of experience, all that training and caution, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe that means something terrible about me. Like maybe I’m not the safe person I thought I was. Maybe I’m actually a danger to people who work beside me, or people who live under my roof.\n\nSo yes, it’s in conflict with how I want to see myself — how I used to see myself. But the thought feels so strong that it’s like it’s trying to rewrite that whole image of who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to neutralize or counteract these thoughts about being a danger to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “neutralize.” I’m not even totally sure what that would look like for me.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific thing I do, like I say a phrase or check something a certain number of times and then the thought gets canceled out. I don’t really have that kind of clear before-and-after feeling. It’s more messy than that. The thought comes in — that I could be dangerous, or that I already missed something important — and then I feel like I have to somehow answer it. Like I can’t just leave it sitting there unchallenged, because if I leave it alone, it feels like I’m accepting that it might be true.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with it, I guess. I’ll think through what happened, what I know about myself, whether I’m actually that kind of person, whether there’s evidence for it or against it. But I don’t know if that counts as counteracting it, because it doesn’t really make it stop. Sometimes it just turns into another loop, where I’m trying to prove to myself I’m not a danger, but every answer has some little “what if” attached to it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel the urge to do something in my head, to push back against it somehow. But I don’t have a specific thing that neutralizes it. If anything, I’m usually left still unsure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something you'd be able to stop thinking about if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could. I’ve tried to just tell myself, “Stop thinking about it, move on, you know who you are,” but it doesn’t work like that.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shake this thought that if all my years doing electrical work — all the training, all the habits, all the ways I thought I knew how to keep things safe — if that still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? It starts turning into this thing where I think, maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Or anybody who lives under the same roof as me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds… I don’t know, extreme maybe. But it doesn’t feel like a thought I can just set down because I want to. It keeps coming back, and when it comes back it feels important, like I’m not allowed to ignore it. Like ignoring it would be careless. So no, I don’t feel like I can just stop thinking about it on command. If I could, I don’t think I’d be sitting here talking about it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are consistent with your own beliefs and values about yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not really. That’s what makes it feel so wrong and so painful. It doesn’t fit with what I used to believe about myself at all.\n\nI’ve spent my whole working life trying to be the opposite of dangerous. As an electrician, you don’t survive by being careless, and you definitely don’t keep other people safe by being reckless. I took pride in knowing what I was doing, checking things, thinking ahead, not cutting corners. I believed I was someone people could trust around that kind of work.\n\nBut now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years doing the job — all the experience and caution and training — still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not who I thought I was. Maybe I’m not safe to be around. Maybe I’m a danger to anyone who works with me, or even people living under the same roof as me.\n\nSo no, it’s not consistent with my values. My values are to protect people, to be responsible, to not put anyone at risk. But the thought keeps coming in anyway, like it’s arguing that none of that matters because something terrible still happened. And even though part of me knows that’s not how I want to see myself, another part of me keeps asking, “What if it’s true?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something that you can recognize as not being a part of your own beliefs, but are still present in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s exactly the hard part. I can recognize, at least part of me can, that this isn’t how I used to think about myself. It doesn’t feel like one of my actual values or beliefs, like something I would calmly say is true if I were thinking straight.\n\nI mean, I built my whole life around trying not to be a danger to people. That’s part of being an electrician — you’re always thinking about what could hurt someone, what could go wrong, how to prevent it. So the idea that I’m dangerous, that I shouldn’t be trusted around people I work with or even people under my own roof… that doesn’t feel like it belongs with who I thought I was.\n\nBut it’s still there. That’s why I came in. I can’t shake the thought that if all my years doing electrical work, all that experience and caution, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives with me. And even when I can say, “That’s not really what I believe,” it doesn’t just leave. It keeps sitting there in my mind like it might be true.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… yes. I think that’s fair to say. It does feel obsessive, at least the way you’re describing it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, now I’m going to think about how dangerous I am.” I don’t want to think it. I hate thinking it. But it keeps coming back anyway.

I’ll be trying to do something normal, or even just sitting there, and then the thought comes in again — if all my years as an electrician, all that training and caution and checking and knowing how to keep people safe, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe… then maybe I’m dangerous. Maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted under the same roof with people. That’s part of why I came in. I can’t shake it. It feels unwanted, but also like I’m not allowed to ignore it, if that makes sense. Like ignoring it would mean I’m pretending there isn’t a risk. So yes, it repeats, it pushes itself into my head, and even when I don’t want to go there, I feel pulled into thinking about it.

That’s part of what makes it so hard to live with, I think. Because I never saw myself that way. I’ve always thought of myself as careful, responsible — especially with electrical work. That’s not a job where you get to be casual. You build your whole way of working around not taking chances, checking things, thinking ahead, making sure the people around you are safe.\n\nAnd now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years of experience, all that training and caution, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe that means something terrible about me. Like maybe I’m not the safe person I thought I was. Maybe I’m actually a danger to people who work beside me, or people who live under my roof.\n\nSo yes, it’s in conflict with how I want to see myself — how I used to see myself. But the thought feels so strong that it’s like it’s trying to rewrite that whole image of who I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to neutralize or counteract these thoughts about being a danger to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “neutralize.” I’m not even totally sure what that would look like for me.\n\nIt’s not like I have a specific thing I do, like I say a phrase or check something a certain number of times and then the thought gets canceled out. I don’t really have that kind of clear before-and-after feeling. It’s more messy than that. The thought comes in — that I could be dangerous, or that I already missed something important — and then I feel like I have to somehow answer it. Like I can’t just leave it sitting there unchallenged, because if I leave it alone, it feels like I’m accepting that it might be true.\n\nSo I’ll try to reason with it, I guess. I’ll think through what happened, what I know about myself, whether I’m actually that kind of person, whether there’s evidence for it or against it. But I don’t know if that counts as counteracting it, because it doesn’t really make it stop. Sometimes it just turns into another loop, where I’m trying to prove to myself I’m not a danger, but every answer has some little “what if” attached to it.\n\nSo… I guess I feel the urge to do something in my head, to push back against it somehow. But I don’t have a specific thing that neutralizes it. If anything, I’m usually left still unsure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something you'd be able to stop thinking about if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I wish I could. I’ve tried to just tell myself, “Stop thinking about it, move on, you know who you are,” but it doesn’t work like that.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I can’t shake this thought that if all my years doing electrical work — all the training, all the habits, all the ways I thought I knew how to keep things safe — if that still wasn’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? It starts turning into this thing where I think, maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Or anybody who lives under the same roof as me.\n\nAnd I know that sounds… I don’t know, extreme maybe. But it doesn’t feel like a thought I can just set down because I want to. It keeps coming back, and when it comes back it feels important, like I’m not allowed to ignore it. Like ignoring it would be careless. So no, I don’t feel like I can just stop thinking about it on command. If I could, I don’t think I’d be sitting here talking about it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are consistent with your own beliefs and values about yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not really. That’s what makes it feel so wrong and so painful. It doesn’t fit with what I used to believe about myself at all.\n\nI’ve spent my whole working life trying to be the opposite of dangerous. As an electrician, you don’t survive by being careless, and you definitely don’t keep other people safe by being reckless. I took pride in knowing what I was doing, checking things, thinking ahead, not cutting corners. I believed I was someone people could trust around that kind of work.\n\nBut now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years doing the job — all the experience and caution and training — still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not who I thought I was. Maybe I’m not safe to be around. Maybe I’m a danger to anyone who works with me, or even people living under the same roof as me.\n\nSo no, it’s not consistent with my values. My values are to protect people, to be responsible, to not put anyone at risk. But the thought keeps coming in anyway, like it’s arguing that none of that matters because something terrible still happened. And even though part of me knows that’s not how I want to see myself, another part of me keeps asking, “What if it’s true?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something that you can recognize as not being a part of your own beliefs, but are still present in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s exactly the hard part. I can recognize, at least part of me can, that this isn’t how I used to think about myself. It doesn’t feel like one of my actual values or beliefs, like something I would calmly say is true if I were thinking straight.\n\nI mean, I built my whole life around trying not to be a danger to people. That’s part of being an electrician — you’re always thinking about what could hurt someone, what could go wrong, how to prevent it. So the idea that I’m dangerous, that I shouldn’t be trusted around people I work with or even people under my own roof… that doesn’t feel like it belongs with who I thought I was.\n\nBut it’s still there. That’s why I came in. I can’t shake the thought that if all my years doing electrical work, all that experience and caution, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives with me. And even when I can say, “That’s not really what I believe,” it doesn’t just leave. It keeps sitting there in my mind like it might be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are obsessive, meaning they are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you feel compelled to think about them even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I think that’s fair to say. It does feel obsessive, at least the way you’re describing it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, now I’m going to think about how dangerous I am.” I don’t want to think it. I hate thinking it. But it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nI’ll be trying to do something normal, or even just sitting there, and then the thought comes in again — if all my years as an electrician, all that training and caution and checking and knowing how to keep people safe, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe… then maybe I’m dangerous. Maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted under the same roof with people. And once it starts, it’s like I have to follow it through, like if I don’t think about it enough then I’m being careless again.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I can’t shake it. It feels unwanted, but also like I’m not allowed to ignore it, if that makes sense. Like ignoring it would mean I’m pretending there isn’t a risk. So yes, it repeats, it pushes itself into my head, and even when I don’t want to go there, I feel pulled into thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No… not in line with who I thought I was. That’s the part that’s been tearing me up.

My whole sense of myself was built around being careful, responsible, the guy who knew what he was doing. As an electrician, you’re trained to think ahead, to double-check, to not take chances, because people can get hurt if you’re sloppy. I took that seriously. I thought I was someone people could safely work with, someone they could trust.

But now this thought just won’t let go. I came in because I can’t shake it — that if all those years doing electrical work, all the caution and experience I had, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anybody who lives under my roof.

And I don’t want that to be my identity. I don’t want to see myself that way. But it keeps pushing in like… maybe this is who I really am and I just didn’t know it before. So no, it doesn’t feel like the self I used to know, but it’s starting to get tangled up with how I see myself now, and that scares me.

And then it turns into this bigger thing, like... if I couldn’t keep him safe with all the years I’ve spent doing this work, all the training and experience and checking things over, then maybe the problem is me.\n\nAnd that thought doesn’t stay contained. It just shows up.\n\nAnd it’s always that same kind of thought, just wearing a different face. If all my years doing electrical work — all the safety checks, all the experience, all the things I’m supposed to know — if that still didn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? It feels like it grabs the wheel before I even realize I’m thinking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific, like a ritual or a check, to try and make these thoughts about danger go away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a ritual exactly. That’s where it gets hard for me to even answer clearly, because what’s normal checking and what’s me trying to quiet my head? It’s more... I’m trying to get relief, I guess. Trying to get that feeling to back off for a second. But even when I check, it doesn’t really make the thought go away. It might quiet down for a minute, and then my brain finds another angle.\n\nSo, specific ritual? I don’t think so. Not in the way you’re asking, maybe. It’s more that I get stuck questioning myself. Like, “Did I miss something? Did I really make sure?” And then I’ll look again, or think through it again, but I don’t have some exact thing I do every time that makes it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about danger and responsibility are unwanted and are invading your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah... unwanted is exactly the word. People trust you with things they don’t even see behind the walls.\n\nBut now... it feels like part of me does agree with it, or at least I can’t argue it down. That’s why I came in. I don’t want that to be true. I don’t want to believe I’m dangerous. But the thought feels... convincing. Like there’s evidence for it that I can’t get around. So it’s both, I guess. It goes against how I want to see myself, but right now it feels like something I can’t stop believing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are in line with your own values and beliefs about yourself?\n\nPatient: No... I mean, not in line with the values I thought I had, anyway. I never wanted to be someone people had to be afraid of. My whole working life, being an electrician, I tried to be the opposite of that. Careful. Responsible. The person who checks things because people are depending on him.\n\nBut that’s what makes it so hard to answer, because now the thought feels like it’s attached to me. I came in because I can’t shake it — that if all my years doing this work, all the training and experience, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not the careful person I believed I was. Maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works beside me, or anybody who lives under my roof.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t match what I want my values to be. It goes against everything I used to believe about myself. I get caught trying to prove to myself that I’m not dangerous, but I don’t know that I have a set thing I do that makes the thought go away. I’ll think through it over and over, like reviewing what kind of person I am, what I would or wouldn’t do, whether I can trust my own judgment. But no, I haven’t noticed any specific thing I have to perform to counteract it. It’s more like I’m stuck arguing with myself, and the argument never really ends.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions over and over to try and make these thoughts about danger go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying it, like repeating an action over and over until the thought goes away. I haven’t noticed myself doing something like checking a door ten times or touching something a certain number of times, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWhat happens more is I get stuck in my head. I’ll go over the same question again and again — am I dangerous, can people trust me, did I miss something about myself — but it’s not like there’s a repeated action that makes it stop. But it doesn’t really settle anything. It’s not like I reanalyze it and then feel clean or safe afterward. If anything, I can go through it ten different ways and still end up right back at the same question.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do mentally replay and reanalyze, but I don’t know that I do it as a deliberate way to make the thoughts disappear. It’s more like my mind won’t let the subject drop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts about being a danger to others, to try and prevent any harm from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think I have some set thing I do where I think, “If I do this, then nobody will get hurt.” Not like a rule or a ritual, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nWhen the thought hits, I do feel this pressure in my chest, like I need to respond somehow, like I can’t just ignore it because ignoring it feels irresponsible. But I don’t know that I could point to one specific action I always take. It’s more that I get caught trying to figure out whether I’m safe to be around, whether I can trust myself, whether I’m missing something obvious that everyone else can’t see.\n\nSo… I guess I feel like I should do something, but I don’t really know what that “something” is. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern where I do an action and then feel like harm has been prevented. It’s more just this awful sense that I need to make absolutely sure nothing bad happens, and I don’t know how to get that certainty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are in conflict with your own self-image?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s part of what makes it so hard to live with, I think. Because I never saw myself that way. I’ve always thought of myself as careful, responsible — especially with electrical work. That’s not a job where you get to be casual. You build your whole way of working around not taking chances, checking things, thinking ahead, making sure the people around you are safe.\n\nAnd now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years of experience, all that training and caution, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe that means something terrible about me. Like maybe I’m not the safe person I thought I was. Maybe I’m actually a danger to people who work beside me, or people who live under my roof.\n\nSo yes, it’s in conflict with how I want to see myself — how I used to see myself. If I could, I don’t think I’d be sitting here talking about it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are consistent with your own beliefs and values about yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, not really. That’s what makes it feel so wrong and so painful. It doesn’t fit with what I used to believe about myself at all.\n\nI’ve spent my whole working life trying to be the opposite of dangerous. As an electrician, you don’t survive by being careless, and you definitely don’t keep other people safe by being reckless. I took pride in knowing what I was doing, checking things, thinking ahead, not cutting corners. I believed I was someone people could trust around that kind of work.\n\nBut now I can’t shake this thought that if all those years doing the job — all the experience and caution and training — still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not who I thought I was. Maybe I’m not safe to be around. Maybe I’m a danger to anyone who works with me, or even people living under the same roof as me.\n\nSo no, it’s not consistent with my values. My values are to protect people, to be responsible, to not put anyone at risk. But the thought keeps coming in anyway, like it’s arguing that none of that matters because something terrible still happened. And even though part of me knows that’s not how I want to see myself, another part of me keeps asking, “What if it’s true?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are something that you can recognize as not being a part of your own beliefs, but are still present in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s exactly the hard part. I can recognize, at least part of me can, that this isn’t how I used to think about myself. It doesn’t feel like one of my actual values or beliefs, like something I would calmly say is true if I were thinking straight.\n\nI mean, I built my whole life around trying not to be a danger to people. That’s part of being an electrician — you’re always thinking about what could hurt someone, what could go wrong, how to prevent it. So the idea that I’m dangerous, that I shouldn’t be trusted around people I work with or even people under my own roof… that doesn’t feel like it belongs with who I thought I was.\n\nBut it’s still there. That’s why I came in. I can’t shake the thought that if all my years doing electrical work, all that experience and caution, couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me or lives with me. And even when I can say, “That’s not really what I believe,” it doesn’t just leave. It keeps sitting there in my mind like it might be true.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are obsessive, meaning they are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you feel compelled to think about them even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I think that’s fair to say. It does feel obsessive, at least the way you’re describing it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, now I’m going to think about how dangerous I am.” I don’t want to think it. I hate thinking it. But it keeps coming back anyway.\n\nI’ll be trying to do something normal, or even just sitting there, and then the thought comes in again — if all my years as an electrician, all that training and caution and checking and knowing how to keep people safe, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe… then maybe I’m dangerous. Maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted under the same roof with people. And once it starts, it’s like I have to follow it through, like if I don’t think about it enough then I’m being careless again.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in. I can’t shake it. It feels unwanted, but also like I’m not allowed to ignore it, if that makes sense. Like ignoring it would mean I’m pretending there isn’t a risk. So yes, it repeats, it pushes itself into my head, and even when I don’t want to go there, I feel pulled into thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a danger to others are in line with your own sense of identity and self?\n\nPatient: No… not in line with who I thought I was. That’s the part that’s been tearing me up.\n\nMy whole sense of myself was built around being careful, responsible, the guy who knew what he was doing. As an electrician, you’re trained to think ahead, to double-check, to not take chances, because people can get hurt if you’re sloppy. I took that seriously. I thought I was someone people could safely work with, someone they could trust.\n\nBut now this thought just won’t let go. I came in because I can’t shake it — that if all those years doing electrical work, all the caution and experience I had, still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me. Or even anybody who lives under my roof.\n\nAnd I don’t want that to be my identity. I don’t want to see myself that way. But it keeps pushing in like… maybe this is who I really am and I just didn’t know it before. So no, it doesn’t feel like the self I used to know, but it’s starting to get tangled up with how I see myself now, and that scares me."
